People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Yes… I think so. Things I used to actually look forward to feel like these chores I keep putting off. Book club, for example—I’ll have the book sitting right there and somehow not pick it up.

Yeah… that’s actually kind of what pushed me to come in. My eating has been really all over the place.

Some weeks I barely touch dinner, like I’ll make something or sit with everyone and then just sort of move food around. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets out of the cupboard. Not even like I planned to—I just end up there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t know if I’ve counted it neatly as two weeks, but yes, I get these stretches where everything in the house feels gray and heavy. Like I’ll just sit at the kitchen table, staring at the same spot, feeling empty, even if nothing terrible happened that day.

It’s strange because the light in the kitchen changes in the afternoon and usually I notice that, or I’ll think about wiping the counters or making tea, and instead I just sit there. So… yes, down, I guess. Hopeless sometimes. It’s not always dramatic, it’s more like everything has gone flat.

My eating has been really all over the place.\n\nSome weeks I barely touch dinner, like I’ll make something or sit with everyone and then just sort of move food around. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets out of the cupboard. Not even like I planned to—I just end up there.\n\nI don’t know about the weight exactly, I try not to hover around the scale too much, but the appetite part has definitely been… not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or hopeless nearly every day, and it was hard to do even basic things like getting dressed or going to work?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’ve counted it neatly as two weeks, but yes, I get these stretches where everything in the house feels gray and heavy. Like I’ll just sit at the kitchen table, staring at the same spot, feeling empty, even if nothing terrible happened that day.\n\nIt’s strange because the light in the kitchen changes in the afternoon and usually I notice that, or I’ll think about wiping the counters or making tea, and instead I just sit there. So… yes, down, I guess. Hopeless sometimes. It’s not always dramatic, it’s more like everything has gone flat.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas there been a time when you didn’t feel any pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you happy like cooking or tending to your rose beds?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 46 / no_occupation / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"Things I used to look forward to, like book club, cooking, galleries, and tending the rose beds, can just feel like chores I keep putting off.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, that happens. Things I used to actually look forward to, like book club or going to a gallery, now I’ll see them on the calendar and it feels like this heavy errand I should probably cancel.

And cooking too—I used to enjoy chopping things and getting the pan going, especially if the radio was on, but lately it’s like… why is there so much washing and deciding and standing there? Even the rose beds. I keep meaning to tend them, and then I notice the weeds and think I’ll do it tomorrow, and then tomorrow turns into… well, more tomorrows. So yes, it’s not that I hate those things, they just feel like chores I keep putting off.

Yes… I do. It’s not always because something happened, which is the part that makes me feel sort of foolish trying to explain it.

Like I’ll just sit at the kitchen table, staring at the same spot, feeling empty, even if nothing terrible happened that day.\n\nIt’s strange because the light in the kitchen changes in the afternoon and usually I notice that, or I’ll think about wiping the counters or making tea, and instead I just sit there. So… yes, down, I guess. Hopeless sometimes. So yes, it’s not that I hate those things, they just feel like chores I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the time, and it seemed like the sadness wouldn’t go away, even when nothing bad was happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do get that feeling. Not always in some big dramatic way, but I’ll do something small and then it becomes… enormous in my head.

Like, I bought this expensive tube of pigment once—well, not once, I mean, art supplies are just expensive now, it’s ridiculous—and I kept thinking, why did I do that, we didn’t need that, I’m selfish. And then suddenly it’s not about the paint anymore, it’s like I’ve damaged the whole household somehow.

Or if I snap at my spouse, even just a little, I’ll replay it for hours. The exact tone, what I should’ve said, how I’ve made everything worse. I know people have arguments, that’s normal, but in those low times it feels like proof that I’ve let everyone down.

No, I haven’t really noticed myself doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you’ve lost control while eating, like you can’t stop yourself once you start, especially during those times you’re eating late at night?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, sometimes it sort of feels that way, mostly at night. Like even simple things—dinner, cleaning up, going to bed at a reasonable time—start feeling… tangled. I don’t know if that makes sense. I keep thinking if I could just get the days back into order, the eating would follow, but it hasn’t really worked that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like your blog or facilitator training?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Things I used to actually look forward to feel like these chores I keep putting off. I guess I haven’t noticed being unusually exhausted most days, not in a clear way. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets out of the cupboard. Not even like I planned to—I just end up there.\n\nI don’t know about the weight exactly, I try not to hover around the scale too much, but the appetite part has definitely been… not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or hopeless nearly every day, and it was hard to do even basic things like getting dressed or going to work?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’ve counted it neatly as two weeks, but yes, I get these stretches where everything in the house feels gray and heavy. Like I’ll just sit at the kitchen table, staring at the same spot, feeling empty, even if nothing terrible happened that day.\n\nIt’s strange because the light in the kitchen changes in the afternoon and usually I notice that, or I’ll think about wiping the counters or making tea, and instead I just sit there. So… yes, down, I guess. Hopeless sometimes. It’s not always dramatic, it’s more like everything has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you didn’t feel any pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you happy like cooking or tending to your rose beds?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. Things I used to actually look forward to, like book club or going to a gallery, now I’ll see them on the calendar and it feels like this heavy errand I should probably cancel.\n\nAnd cooking too—I used to enjoy chopping things and getting the pan going, especially if the radio was on, but lately it’s like… why is there so much washing and deciding and standing there? Even the rose beds. I keep meaning to tend them, and then I notice the weeds and think I’ll do it tomorrow, and then tomorrow turns into… well, more tomorrows. So yes, it’s not that I hate those things, they just feel like chores I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the time, and it seemed like the sadness wouldn’t go away, even when nothing bad was happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s not always because something happened, which is the part that makes me feel sort of foolish trying to explain it.\n\nI’ll have these stretches where the whole house just feels gray and heavy, like even the walls are tired. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just… sit there, feeling empty. Sometimes the kettle’s right there, or there are dishes I could do, and I’ll notice little things like a crumb on the counter, but I can’t seem to move myself into the day.\n\nAnd nothing terrible has to have happened. It’s just there. Like the sadness settles in and doesn’t really lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or felt fatigued, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I don’t think of it as being tired exactly, like after carrying groceries or scrubbing the floor. It’s more that I’ll look at something and sort of put it off, but I don’t know if that’s energy or just… not getting started.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m just slow in the mornings. My mother used to say I moved like I was underwater, even when I was a kid, but she said a lot of things. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed fatigue in the way you mean, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you’re a failure or that you’ve let yourself or others down, especially during these low periods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do get that feeling. Not always in some big dramatic way, but I’ll do something small and then it becomes… enormous in my head.\n\nLike, I bought this expensive tube of pigment once—well, not once, I mean, art supplies are just expensive now, it’s ridiculous—and I kept thinking, why did I do that, we didn’t need that, I’m selfish. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I know I already sort of said it, but yes. It’s those little things that turn into a whole court case in my head.

Like the paint—this one tube of pigment, which sounds so silly because people buy things, you know, people buy coffee and shoes and nobody calls a tribunal over it—but I’ll sit there thinking I’ve been selfish, like I’ve somehow ruined the budget or made life harder for everyone. And then if I snap at my spouse, even just a sharp tone, I’ll replay it for hours. What I said, how my face looked, whether I sounded cruel.

It becomes this feeling like I haven’t just made a small mistake, I’ve… I don’t know, damaged the whole household somehow.

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d say nearly every day. I mean, I have days where I sit down and then it’s like the chair has decided I live there now, but that might just be me avoiding things.

Fatigue is funny, because my spouse will say, “Are you tired?” and I’ll say no, because I don’t feel sleepy exactly. It’s more like… the sink is full of cups, and I look at it, and then I start thinking about how we have too many mugs, and how one of them has a crack, and then I’m online looking up whether ceramic glaze is toxic—so it’s not really energy, maybe it’s distraction.

So, no, I haven’t clearly noticed being physically tired or drained nearly every day. Not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.

Like even simple things—dinner, cleaning up, going to bed at a reasonable time—start feeling… tangled. I don’t know if that makes sense. I keep thinking if I could just get the days back into order, the eating would follow, but it hasn’t really worked that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like your blog or facilitator training?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Things I used to actually look forward to feel like these chores I keep putting off. Book club, for example—I’ll have the book sitting right there and somehow not pick it up.\n\nAnd cooking, which used to be sort of calming for me, now feels like this whole production. Even going to galleries, or tending the rose beds outside… I used to love checking on them. My grandmother had roses too, these really stubborn pink ones that survived everything—anyway. Now I’ll look at mine and think, “I should do that,” and then I just don’t. It’s like the interest got dulled down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a big drop in your energy level or felt unusually tired most days, even when you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a big drop, exactly. Some days I feel kind of worn out, but I have trouble telling if that’s actual tiredness or just… not getting myself moving.\n\nLike yesterday I stood in front of a pile of towels for way too long because I couldn’t decide where to start, and then I got distracted by this loose thread on one of them—sorry, that’s not really the question. I guess I haven’t noticed being unusually exhausted most days, not in a clear way. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently, especially when you’re feeling down or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually kind of what pushed me to come in. My eating has been really all over the place.\n\nSome weeks I barely touch dinner, like I’ll make something or sit with everyone and then just sort of move food around. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets out of the cupboard. Not even like I planned to—I just end up there.\n\nI don’t know about the weight exactly, I try not to hover around the scale too much, but the appetite part has definitely been… not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or hopeless nearly every day, and it was hard to do even basic things like getting dressed or going to work?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’ve counted it neatly as two weeks, but yes, I get these stretches where everything in the house feels gray and heavy. Like I’ll just sit at the kitchen table, staring at the same spot, feeling empty, even if nothing terrible happened that day.\n\nIt’s strange because the light in the kitchen changes in the afternoon and usually I notice that, or I’ll think about wiping the counters or making tea, and instead I just sit there. So… yes, down, I guess. Hopeless sometimes. It’s not always dramatic, it’s more like everything has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you didn’t feel any pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you happy like cooking or tending to your rose beds?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. Things I used to actually look forward to, like book club or going to a gallery, now I’ll see them on the calendar and it feels like this heavy errand I should probably cancel.\n\nAnd cooking too—I used to enjoy chopping things and getting the pan going, especially if the radio was on, but lately it’s like… why is there so much washing and deciding and standing there? Even the rose beds. I keep meaning to tend them, and then I notice the weeds and think I’ll do it tomorrow, and then tomorrow turns into… well, more tomorrows. So yes, it’s not that I hate those things, they just feel like chores I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the time, and it seemed like the sadness wouldn’t go away, even when nothing bad was happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s not always because something happened, which is the part that makes me feel sort of foolish trying to explain it.\n\nI’ll have these stretches where the whole house just feels gray and heavy, like even the walls are tired. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just… sit there, feeling empty. Sometimes the kettle’s right there, or there are dishes I could do, and I’ll notice little things like a crumb on the counter, but I can’t seem to move myself into the day.\n\nAnd nothing terrible has to have happened. It’s just there. high or low, but I still felt sure people online were tracking my garden photos and I still heard brief remarks when I was alone.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve fallen behind on my facilitator training and private blog work because my projects and explanations became hard to organize, and the tension at home has made it harder to keep normal routines with my husband.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two years and five months, since the arguments at home over spending and my art projects really started escalating.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 6 months\n  manifestation: The warning messages and hearing brief voices have been happening for about a year and a half now.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: The energized, overcommitted stretches have been part of my life for about a year and eight months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: The gray, guilty periods where I lose interest in things have added up to about seven months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 46 / no_occupation / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you’re a failure or that you’ve let yourself or others down, especially during these low periods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do get that feeling. Not always in some big dramatic way, but I’ll do something small and then it becomes… enormous in my head.\n\nLike, I bought this expensive tube of pigment once—well, not once, I mean, art supplies are just expensive now, it’s ridiculous—and I kept thinking, why did I do that, we didn’t need that, I’m selfish. And then suddenly it’s not about the paint anymore, it’s like I’ve damaged the whole household somehow.\n\nOr if I snap at my spouse, even just a little, I’ll replay it for hours. The exact tone, what I should’ve said, how I’ve made everything worse. I know people have arguments, that’s normal, but in those low times it feels like proof that I’ve let everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you’re a failure or that you’ve let yourself or others down, especially during these low periods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know I already sort of said it, but yes. It’s those little things that turn into a whole court case in my head.\n\nLike the paint—this one tube of pigment, which sounds so silly because people buy things, you know, people buy coffee and shoes and nobody calls a tribunal over it—but I’ll sit there thinking I’ve been selfish, like I’ve somehow ruined the budget or made life harder for everyone. And then if I snap at my spouse, even just a sharp tone, I’ll replay it for hours. What I said, how my face looked, whether I sounded cruel.\n\nIt becomes this feeling like I haven’t just made a small mistake, I’ve… I don’t know, damaged the whole household somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say nearly every day. I mean, I have days where I sit down and then it’s like the chair has decided I live there now, but that might just be me avoiding things.\n\nFatigue is funny, because my spouse will say, “Are you tired?” and I’ll say no, because I don’t feel sleepy exactly. It’s more like… the sink is full of cups, and I look at it, and then I start thinking about how we have too many mugs, and how one of them has a crack, and then I’m online looking up whether ceramic glaze is toxic—so it’s not really energy, maybe it’s distraction.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t clearly noticed being physically tired or drained nearly every day. Not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the time, and it seemed like the sadness wouldn’t go away, even when nothing bad was happening?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yes… there are stretches like that. Not necessarily because something happened, that’s the strange part.

Even the rose beds. I keep meaning to tend them, and then I notice the weeds and think I’ll do it tomorrow, and then tomorrow turns into… well, more tomorrows. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently, especially when you’re feeling down or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually kind of what pushed me to come in. My eating has been really all over the place.\n\nSome weeks I barely touch dinner, like I’ll make something or sit with everyone and then just sort of move food around. And then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’m standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets out of the cupboard. Not even like I planned to—I just end up there.\n\nI don’t know about the weight exactly, I try not to hover around the scale too much, but the appetite part has definitely been… not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or hopeless nearly every day, and it was hard to do even basic things like getting dressed or going to work?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’ve counted it neatly as two weeks, but yes, I get these stretches where everything in the house feels gray and heavy. Like I’ll just sit at the kitchen table, staring at the same spot, feeling empty, even if nothing terrible happened that day.\n\nIt’s strange because the light in the kitchen changes in the afternoon and usually I notice that, or I’ll think about wiping the counters or making tea, and instead I just sit there. So… yes, down, I guess. Hopeless sometimes. It’s not always dramatic, it’s more like everything has gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you didn’t feel any pleasure or interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you happy like cooking or tending to your rose beds?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that happens. Things I used to actually look forward to, like book club or going to a gallery, now I’ll see them on the calendar and it feels like this heavy errand I should probably cancel.\n\nAnd cooking too—I used to enjoy chopping things and getting the pan going, especially if the radio was on, but lately it’s like… why is there so much washing and deciding and standing there? Even the rose beds. I keep meaning to tend them, and then I notice the weeds and think I’ll do it tomorrow, and then tomorrow turns into… well, more tomorrows. So yes, it’s not that I hate those things, they just feel like chores I keep putting off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the time, and it seemed like the sadness wouldn’t go away, even when nothing bad was happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s not always because something happened, which is the part that makes me feel sort of foolish trying to explain it.\n\nI’ll have these stretches where the whole house just feels gray and heavy, like even the walls are tired. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just… sit there, feeling empty. Sometimes the kettle’s right there, or there are dishes I could do, and I’ll notice little things like a crumb on the counter, but I can’t seem to move myself into the day.\n\nAnd nothing terrible has to have happened. It’s just there. Like the sadness settles in and doesn’t really lift.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or felt fatigued, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I don’t think of it as being tired exactly, like after carrying groceries or scrubbing the floor. It’s more that I’ll look at something and sort of put it off, but I don’t know if that’s energy or just… not getting started.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I’m just slow in the mornings. My mother used to say I moved like I was underwater, even when I was a kid, but she said a lot of things. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed fatigue in the way you mean, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you’re a failure or that you’ve let yourself or others down, especially during these low periods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do get that feeling. Not always in some big dramatic way, but I’ll do something small and then it becomes… enormous in my head.\n\nLike, I bought this expensive tube of pigment once—well, not once, I mean, art supplies are just expensive now, it’s ridiculous—and I kept thinking, why did I do that, we didn’t need that, I’m selfish. And then suddenly it’s not about the paint anymore, it’s like I’ve damaged the whole household somehow.\n\nOr if I snap at my spouse, even just a little, I’ll replay it for hours. The exact tone, what I should’ve said, how I’ve made everything worse. I know people have arguments, that’s normal, but in those low times it feels like proof that I’ve let everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you’re a failure or that you’ve let yourself or others down, especially during these low periods?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know I already sort of said it, but yes. It’s those little things that turn into a whole court case in my head.\n\nLike the paint—this one tube of pigment, which sounds so silly because people buy things, you know, people buy coffee and shoes and nobody calls a tribunal over it—but I’ll sit there thinking I’ve been selfish, like I’ve somehow ruined the budget or made life harder for everyone. And then if I snap at my spouse, even just a sharp tone, I’ll replay it for hours. What I said, how my face looked, whether I sounded cruel.\n\nIt becomes this feeling like I haven’t just made a small mistake, I’ve… I don’t know, damaged the whole household somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say nearly every day. I mean, I have days where I sit down and then it’s like the chair has decided I live there now, but that might just be me avoiding things.\n\nFatigue is funny, because my spouse will say, “Are you tired?” and I’ll say no, because I don’t feel sleepy exactly. It’s more like… the sink is full of cups, and I look at it, and then I start thinking about how we have too many mugs, and how one of them has a crack, and then I’m online looking up whether ceramic glaze is toxic—so it’s not really energy, maybe it’s distraction.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t clearly noticed being physically tired or drained nearly every day. Not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the time, and it seemed like the sadness wouldn’t go away, even when nothing bad was happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… there are stretches like that. Not necessarily because something happened, that’s the strange part.\n\nIt’s like the whole house feels gray and heavy, even if the weather is fine or nobody’s upset with me. I’ll end up sitting at the kitchen table just feeling… empty. And then I notice stupid things, like crumbs near the toaster or the way the mail piles up, and I start thinking I should reorganize that drawer with all the rubber bands—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yes, sometimes the sadness just sits there, like it doesn’t need a reason."
